Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me, I really appreciate it. Um, I hope it's okay that I made an appointment for this. I might be making a bigger deal out of it than I should, but... well, I've been having a little bit of trouble lately. 

I guess I just get, um, really antsy? Like, whenever I'm supposed to just sit quietly in my classes or in the radiology suite, I start feeling like I'm almost crawling out of my skin. I've really been trying to just tough it out and be professional, I promise I have, for months now. But no matter how hard I try to be good and stay put, I just end up shifting around in my seat constantly, or pacing, or finding some excuse to get up and move. 

I'm sorry, I know that probably sounds a bit silly, but... is that the right kind of thing to come here for? I just want to make sure I'm not wasting your time.

I just want to make sure I'm not wasting your time.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so? I mean, I hope I'm answering that right, but I actually feel like my focus is pretty okay. \n\nI always make sure to listen carefully to my instructors and the senior techs, I really do. I don't want to be a bad student or anything, so I try really hard to pay attention to everything they say. It's just... it's really just my body wanting to move around, even when my brain is completely tuned in to the lecture or the scans. \n\nDoes that... does that make sense? But no matter how hard I try to be good and stay put, I just end up shifting around in my seat constantly, or pacing, or finding some excuse to get up and move. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that probably sounds a bit silly, but... is that the right kind of thing to come here for? I just want to make sure I'm not wasting your time.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so? I mean, I hope I'm answering that right, but I actually feel like my focus is pretty okay. \n\nI always make sure to listen carefully to my instructors and the senior techs, I really do. I don't want to be a bad student or anything, so I try really hard to pay attention to everything they say. It's just... it's really just my body wanting to move around, even when my brain is completely tuned in to the lecture or the scans. \n\nDoes that... does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it okay.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen in more than one place, like at work or at home too?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, like I mentioned, it definitely happens in my classes and at the hospital during my clinicals. But at home... I'm actually not really sure? I hope that's an okay answer. \n\nI guess I just haven't really noticed it as much there. Maybe because when I'm in my own space, I can just kind of... get up and do chores or walk around whenever I want without bothering anybody? I really don't want to make it sound like I'm just completely out of control everywhere I go. I try really hard to keep a lid on it, especially around my family. \n\nIs it... is it normal to only really notice it when you're supposed to be sitting still for a long time? I hope I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people or blurt out answers before they finish asking the question?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I really try my best not to be rude or anything like that. I respect my supervisors and instructors so much. But... I guess, sometimes, yeah? \n\nLike, if a senior tech is explaining a positioning step for a scan, or if they're asking me a question to test my knowledge, I just get so eager to show them that I know what I'm doing. So I'll kind of... jump in and give the answer before they're totally done talking. I just want them to see that I'm learning and paying attention! But then, um, sometimes I realize I interrupted before they got to the part that actually mattered, and I feel terrible about it. \n\nI really don't mean to be disrespectful, I promise. I just get a little too enthusiastic, I think? Is that... does that make me sound like a bad student? I hope you don't think I'm just going around talking over everyone all the time.\n\nDoctor: Did these restlessness and interrupting symptoms start before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yeah, I guess they did. I hope it doesn't make me sound like I was a troublemaker or a bad kid or anything! I was just really energetic, I promise. \n\nBut, um, my parents actually still talk about my old elementary school conferences. They say my teachers would always mention how I was out of my seat a lot, or calling out, or just kind of... rushing ahead to do things before the directions were completely finished. I just always wanted to get started right away. \n\nSo, yeah, that was definitely happening way before middle school. It's not just something that started recently with my radiology training. I've always tried really hard to be a good student, though, even back then! Is that... does that answer your question okay? I really care about being a good tech.\n\nDoctor: Do these fidgety or restless symptoms cause problems for you outside of school or work, such as in your relationships or daily life at home?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, at home, my family is kind of used to it, so it doesn't cause huge fights or anything. Oh, um, organizing tasks? No, I... I don't think I have much trouble with that. I mean, I really try my best to keep everything in order.

But then, um, sometimes I realize I interrupted before they got to the part that actually mattered, and I feel terrible about it. \n\nI really don't mean to be disrespectful, I promise. I just get a little too enthusiastic, I think? Is that... does that make me sound like a bad student? I hope you don't think I'm just going around talking over everyone all the time.\n\nDoctor: Did these restlessness and interrupting symptoms start before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yeah, I guess they did. I hope it doesn't make me sound like I was a troublemaker or a bad kid or anything! I was just really energetic, I promise. \n\nBut, um, my parents actually still talk about my old elementary school conferences. They say my teachers would always mention how I was out of my seat a lot, or calling out, or just kind of... rushing ahead to do things before the directions were completely finished. I just always wanted to get started right away. \n\nSo, yeah, that was definitely happening way before middle school. It's not just something that started recently with my radiology training. I've always tried really hard to be a good student, though, even back then! Is that... does that answer your question okay? I hope that's the kind of information you needed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety enough that you have trouble staying seated when you’re expected to?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yeah, I guess that's actually the main reason I decided to come in. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... trouble with details or careless mistakes? I... I really don't think so. I try to be so, so careful, especially at the clinic. 

You know, in radiology, details are everything, so I always make sure to double-check my work. I mean, I guess everyone makes a tiny mistake here or there when they're first learning, right? But I always try to catch myself before it becomes an issue. I really want the senior techs to know they can count on me, so I put a lot of effort into being accurate. 

I don't think I've ever had, like, a *lot* of trouble with it. I always just want to do a good job and not mess anything up for the patients. Does... does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it right.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.1, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm Dr. ____. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me, I really appreciate it. Um, I hope it's okay that I made an appointment for this. I might be making a bigger deal out of it than I should, but... well, I've been having a little bit of trouble lately. \n\nI guess I just get, um, really antsy? Like, whenever I'm supposed to just sit quietly in my classes or in the radiology suite, I start feeling like I'm almost crawling out of my skin. I've really been trying to just tough it out and be professional, I promise I have, for months now. But no matter how hard I try to be good and stay put, I just end up shifting around in my seat constantly, or pacing, or finding some excuse to get up and move. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that probably sounds a bit silly, but... is that the right kind of thing to come here for? I just want to make sure I'm not wasting your time.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so? I mean, I hope I'm answering that right, but I actually feel like my focus is pretty okay. \n\nI always make sure to listen carefully to my instructors and the senior techs, I really do. I don't want to be a bad student or anything, so I try really hard to pay attention to everything they say. It's just... it's really just my body wanting to move around, even when my brain is completely tuned in to the lecture or the scans. \n\nDoes that... does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it okay.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen in more than one place, like at work or at home too?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, like I mentioned, it definitely happens in my classes and at the hospital during my clinicals. But at home... I'm actually not really sure? I hope that's an okay answer. \n\nI guess I just haven't really noticed it as much there. Maybe because when I'm in my own space, I can just kind of... get up and do chores or walk around whenever I want without bothering anybody? I really don't want to make it sound like I'm just completely out of control everywhere I go. I try really hard to keep a lid on it, especially around my family. \n\nIs it... is it normal to only really notice it when you're supposed to be sitting still for a long time? I hope I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people or blurt out answers before they finish asking the question?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I really try my best not to be rude or anything like that. I respect my supervisors and instructors so much. But... I guess, sometimes, yeah? \n\nLike, if a senior tech is explaining a positioning step for a scan, or if they're asking me a question to test my knowledge, I just get so eager to show them that I know what I'm doing. So I'll kind of... jump in and give the answer before they're totally done talking. I just want them to see that I'm learning and paying attention! But then, um, sometimes I realize I interrupted before they got to the part that actually mattered, and I feel terrible about it. \n\nI really don't mean to be disrespectful, I promise. I just get a little too enthusiastic, I think? Is that... does that make me sound like a bad student? I hope you don't think I'm just going around talking over everyone all the time.\n\nDoctor: Did these restlessness and interrupting symptoms start before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yeah, I guess they did. I hope it doesn't make me sound like I was a troublemaker or a bad kid or anything! I was just really energetic, I promise. \n\nBut, um, my parents actually still talk about my old elementary school conferences. They say my teachers would always mention how I was out of my seat a lot, or calling out, or just kind of... rushing ahead to do things before the directions were completely finished. I just always wanted to get started right away. \n\nSo, yeah, that was definitely happening way before middle school. It's not just something that started recently with my radiology training. I've always tried really hard to be a good student, though, even back then! Is that... does that answer your question okay? I hope that's the kind of information you needed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety enough that you have trouble staying seated when you’re expected to?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yeah, I guess that's actually the main reason I decided to come in. I really hope I'm not making a bigger deal out of it than I should be, but... it's just been really hard. \n\nWhen I'm in my radiology classes, or especially when I'm in the suite and I'm just supposed to be sitting there observing or waiting for the next patient... I just feel this awful feeling. Like I'm practically crawling out of my skin. I try so, so hard to just tough it out and be professional, I really do! I've been trying for months to just push through it. \n\nBut I almost always end up shifting around in my chair a ton, or pacing the room, or... or I'll just find some random excuse to get up and move, like pretending I need to check on some supplies or something. I just can't seem to stay put. Is that... is it okay that I do that? I just really don't want my instructors to think I'm not taking the program seriously. I really care about being a good tech.\n\nDoctor: Do these fidgety or restless symptoms cause problems for you outside of school or work, such as in your relationships or daily life at home?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, at home, my family is kind of used to it, so it doesn't cause huge fights or anything. But... um... \n\nI'm actually really embarrassed to admit this. I really hope you don't think I'm a failure or a bad student, because I promise I am trying so, so hard to do everything right. But... the real problem is actually at my clinicals. \n\nMy clinical evaluations have... well, they've taken a pretty big hit lately. And, um... they actually had to put me on a formal remediation plan. I felt so awful when they told me. My supervisors said that my behavior is kind of disrupting the patient flow, and that it makes teamwork a lot harder for everyone else in the department. I really, really never wanted to make things harder for my team! I respect them so much. \n\nI'm just so worried that if I don't get this under control, it could actually cost me my place in the radiology program. I want to do this more than anything, and I'm terrified of messing it all up. Is it... does that make sense why I'm so stressed about it? I just really want to fix it so my instructors see that I'm taking it seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting your turn?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, waiting is... it's a little tough for me sometimes. I really try to be patient, I promise! I know everyone else is busy and trying to learn, too. \n\nBut, um, like during lab practice, or when I'm waiting for one of the machines to open up so I can do my scans... I just get so, so edgy. It's like I can't just stand there quietly. I end up kind of... stepping forward a lot, or checking the time over and over, and I'll keep asking if it's my turn yet or if I can go next. \n\nI don't mean to be pushy at all! I just get so eager to practice. But, um, it has gotten me called out by the instructors more than once. I always apologize right away, of course! I really don't want them to think I'm selfish or that I think my time is more important than the other students'. Do you think... is it normal to get that antsy when you're just really excited to learn? I hope it doesn't sound too bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you often leave your seat when you're supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I guess I sort of already mentioned that earlier, didn't I? I'm so sorry, I hope I'm not repeating myself too much and wasting your time. \n\nBut, um, yes... I do end up leaving my seat a lot when I'm supposed to be sitting. Like I was saying about being in the suite or during lectures, I just... I try really, really hard to stay put, but I always end up finding some reason to get up. I'll pretend I need to check the supply closet, or go to the bathroom, or just pace in the back of the room. \n\nI really don't want to come across as disrespectful to my instructors, you know? I always try to do it quietly so I don't bother anyone. Is it... is it okay that I do that if I'm not making a big scene? I just really want to be a good student.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or miss important details because you lose track of what you’re doing?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... careless mistakes? I... I don't think so. I mean, I really try to pay close attention to everything I'm doing, especially because we're working with patients and, you know, radiation. \n\nI might double-check things a lot just to be absolutely sure, but I don't really lose track of what I'm doing to the point of messing up important details. I always try to be really thorough. I hope that's the right way to handle it? I just really want to make sure I'm doing everything exactly the way the instructors teach us. Does that sound okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or finishing assignments?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, organizing tasks? No, I... I don't think I have much trouble with that. I mean, I really try my best to keep everything in order. \n\nIn the radiology program, there's a lot to keep track of—like clinical hours, assignments, and reading—but I always try to make sure I get it all done. I might, um, you know, have to pull a late night here and there just to make sure it's perfect, but I always finish my assignments. I really don't want to fall behind or let my instructors down. \n\nI try to keep a planner and write everything down so I don't miss anything. That's... that's a good way to handle it, right? I just want to make sure I'm staying on top of everything the way I'm supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk too much?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... talk too much? I... I really hope not. I try very hard to be a good listener. Especially in the clinic, you know, it's so important to let the patients and the senior techs do the talking. I always try to just pay attention and be respectful. \n\nUm... I hope I haven't been talking your ear off today? I know I've been giving kind of long answers, but I just want to make sure I'm giving you exactly what you need. I really try to be mindful of not interrupting people or taking up too much space in conversations. I think I do okay with that... at least, I hope I do. Am I answering your questions okay so far? I just never want to be a bother to anyone.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often lose things you needed for school or daily activities, like pencils, homework, or other belongings?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... losing things when I was younger? I... I really don't think so. I always tried to be very careful with my belongings. \n\nMy parents taught me early on to take good care of my school supplies, so I would always make sure to pack my backpack up perfectly every night before bed. I mean, maybe I misplaced a pencil once in a great while, just like any kid, but never anything major like my homework or important folders. I always wanted to be responsible and not cause any extra stress for my teachers or my parents. \n\nThat's... that's pretty normal, right? I just always tried my best to keep track of what was mine so I wouldn't be a burden. I hope I'm remembering it all okay for you.\n\nDoctor: Were these same behaviors also noticed at home by your parents or family when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... at home? Well... I guess, yeah, my parents did know about some of that stuff early on. They... they actually still bring up my old elementary school conferences sometimes. \n\nUm, the teachers used to tell them that I was, you know, always out of my seat, or that I'd call out, or kind of rush ahead before they even finished giving the directions. I... I really didn't mean to be disruptive, I promise. I think I just got a little too eager to do the work, you know? But, yeah, that was happening way before middle school... so it's definitely not just something that started recently with my radiology training. \n\nI hope that's okay to share? I just really don't want you to think I was a bad kid or anything... I always tried my absolute best to be good for my parents and my teachers. Is... is that the kind of information you needed?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a lot of trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes at school or work?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... trouble with details or careless mistakes? I... I really don't think so. I try to be so, so careful, especially at the clinic. \n\nYou know, in radiology, details are everything, so I always make sure to double-check my work. I mean, I guess everyone makes a tiny mistake here or there when they're first learning, right? But I always try to catch myself before it becomes an issue. I really want the senior techs to know they can count on me, so I put a lot of effort into being accurate. \n\nI don't think I've ever had, like, a *lot* of trouble with it. I always just want to do a good job and not mess anything up for the patients. Does... does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining your attention when you’re reading, studying, or listening to a lecture?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... trouble keeping my attention on things? I... I don't really think so. I mean, studying for my radiology classes can be tough sometimes, but I always try really hard to focus. \n\nI guess sometimes if a lecture is super long, my mind might wander for just a second, but that happens to everyone, right? I always make sure to pull myself right back so I don't miss anything important. I really want to do well in my program, so I try to be as attentive as possible. I've never had any major issues with it, I promise. I always just try my best to be a good student. \n\nIs... is that the right kind of answer? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these restlessness or impulsive behaviors at home often enough that they cause problems there, too?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... at home? Causing problems? I... I don't think they really cause *problems*, per se. \n\nI mean, I know I can be a little energetic sometimes, maybe tapping my foot or pacing a bit when I'm watching TV, but my parents are used to it. They know I just have a lot of energy. I try really hard not to bother anyone or get in their way. If I feel like I'm being too much, I'll just go for a quick run or do some chores to help out around the house. I always want to make sure things are peaceful at home, you know? \n\nI really don't think I'm causing any major issues for them. I try to be as helpful and considerate as I can. Does that... does that sound okay? I just wouldn't want you to think I'm a burden to my family or anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... well, like I said, at home I really try my best to keep things peaceful so I'm not a bother. But... I guess... at my clinicals for the radiology program...

I really care about being a good tech.\n\nDoctor: Do these fidgety or restless symptoms cause problems for you outside of school or work, such as in your relationships or daily life at home?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, at home, my family is kind of used to it, so it doesn't cause huge fights or anything. But... um... \n\nI'm actually really embarrassed to admit this. I really hope you don't think I'm a failure or a bad student, because I promise I am trying so, so hard to do everything right. But... the real problem is actually at my clinicals. \n\nMy clinical evaluations have... well, they've taken a pretty big hit lately. And, um... they actually had to put me on a formal remediation plan. I felt so awful when they told me. My supervisors said that my behavior is kind of disrupting the patient flow, and that it makes teamwork a lot harder for everyone else in the department. I really, really never wanted to make things harder for my team! I respect them so much. \n\nI'm just so worried that if I don't get this under control, it could actually cost me my place in the radiology program. I want to do this more than anything, and I'm terrified of messing it all up. Is it... does that make sense why I'm so stressed about it? I just really want to fix it so my instructors see that I'm taking it seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting your turn?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, waiting is... it's a little tough for me sometimes. I really try to be patient, I promise! It's like I can't just stand there quietly. I end up kind of... stepping forward a lot, or checking the time over and over, and I'll keep asking if it's my turn yet or if I can go next. \n\nI don't mean to be pushy at all! Do you think... is it normal to get that antsy when you're just really excited to learn? I hope it doesn't sound too bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you often leave your seat when you're supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I guess I sort of already mentioned that earlier, didn't I? I'll pretend I need to check the supply closet, or go to the bathroom, or just pace in the back of the room. \n\nI really don't want to come across as disrespectful to my instructors, you know?
